Invest in Germany, Yes, But How?  
Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity

### Page 2

2

• 
 Germany’s investment gap can therefore become one of Europe’s largest investment 
opportunities, in four verticals: energy, digital infrastructure, transport and mobility, 
and defense.  
In energy, needs reach ~EUR1.3trn by 2045 (about 30% of 2025 GDP) for 
renewables, grids, storage and hydrogen. Projects are often too small for institutional 
investors (11,000 local authorities, 800 Stadtwerke), so the enablers are advisory 
support, stand
 ardized tenders and aggregation at district and county level, and low
 -
carbon FDI rose 25% across Europe in 2025. Digital infrastructure needs EUR275
 -
500bn 
by 2030 (6
 -
11% of GDP) for hardware, telecoms, software and data centers, and foreign 
capital is alre
 ady committing, for example Google, Microsoft and Nvidia. The constraint 
is grid connection and permitting, not capital, so connection approvals should be 
prioritized. Transport and mobility 
 has  
EUR166bn planned for 2025
 -
29 (EUR107bn rail, 
EUR52bn federal roads, EUR8bn waterways), with PPP 2.0 as the enabler: performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term offtake contracts that tie returns to 
predictable cash flows. Defense financing i
 s ~EUR734bn over five years, enough to meet 
NATO’s 3.5% of GDP target for core defense by 2029, and defense FDI projects rose 84% 
across Europe in 2025, with simpler regulation and procurement as the enabler. Across 
all four, the common enablers are faster  
 permitting and procurement, grid access, 
standardized structures and performance
 -
based PPPs with long
 -
term offtake contracts. 
Success would lift potential growth and ease pressure on the social state. Failure would 
leave Germany with higher debt and littl
 e improvement in long
 -
term growth.

Germany’s chronic investment shortage despite appealing conditions  
Germany's recent economic weakness should not obscure its enduring strengths and in
 -
principle attractiveness 
for investment.  
After several years of stagnation, the country remains Europe's largest economy, one of the world's 
leading industrial and exporting nations and home to globally competitive companies, world
 -
class research 
institutions, deep pools of skilled labor and exc
 eptionally strong public institutions. For long
 -
term investors, 
Germany offers a rare combination of economic scale, technological capability, political stability and financial 
strength.  
• 
 Scale matters: Germany is the EU's largest and the world's third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP) 
meaning every investment project offers access to a substantial domestic market.  
Germany is also the 
third largest exporter, with one of the world's deepest manufacturing ecosystems, strong in machinery, 
chemicals, electrical equipment, vehicles pharmaceuticals, industrial automation and precision 
engineering, carried by a broad netwo
 rk of medium
 -
sized specialist firms. The 2026 EY attractiveness 
survey ranks Germany as the third most attractive investment destination in Europe, with a narrowing gap 
towards the top spots (France and UK).  
• 
 Its AAA rating and top institutional quality provide a stable framework for investors.  
Germany is the only 
G7 country that enjoys a AAA rating from the top three agencies, indicative of comfortably solid public 
finances. It has the top Rule of Law score in the G20 and ranks among the top 20 out of 208 countries in the 
World Bank’s various G
 overnance Indicators, reflecting strong rule of law, effective institutions, democratic 
accountability and relatively low corruption. This institutional strength provides a predictable and stable 
framework for businesses.  
• 
 The “Mittelstand
 :” 1700 hidden champions beyond the 40 global DAX companies form a uniquely strong 
economic ecosystem.  
Around 25% of German exports originate from medium
 -
sized companies that are 
niche B2B world market leaders. These hidden champions are strong innovators, filing patents at ~5x the 
rate of large corporates per employee. Many are privately held and often fa
 mily
-
owned.  
• 
 Germany is an innovation powerhouse and automation leader.  
Ranked 11th out of 139 economies in the 
2025 WIPO Global Innovation Index, it hosts seven of the world's leading innovation clusters and performs 
strongly in science, patent generation and logistics, where it ranks third globally. It is the world's fourth  
most 
automated manufacturing economy, with 449 industrial robots per 10,000 manufacturing workers (vs. a 
global average of 132). Its commitment to innovation is reflected in R&D spending equivalent to 3.1% of 
GDP, placing it 10th among roughly 80 countrie
 s tracked and ahead of the OECD average of 2.7%. Foreign

### Page 3

3

investors see this similarly: they name the close integration of industry, science and research, legal certainty 
and the skilled workforce as positives of Germany.
 1 
• 
 Human capital and scientific excellence.  
German nationals have won 17 Nobel prizes in scientific fields in 
the last 30 years alone, most recently this year (for medicine on 5 October). Max Planck Society researchers 
have been involved in 31 Nobel prizes since the institution’s founding
 . Three German universities are listed 
in the global top 50 on two different major international rankings: the Technical University of Munich, LMU 
Munich, which scored a perfect 100% on industry connections, and the University of Heidelberg. The country 
al
 so has the highest share of STEM graduates in the OECD.  
• 
 Quality of life and attractiveness for global talent.  
Three German cities make the global top 10 in Mercer’s 
most recent Quality of Living ranking (Frankfurt, Munich and Duesseldorf). Moreover, Germany is not only 
among the frontrunners in tackling climate change but also ranks sixth in Yale’s Environmental 
Performance Index, reinforcing its appeal as a destination for international talent.  
Despite these structural advantages, a massive investment gap has opened. 
 A key macroeconomic fact about 
Germany is its chronic lack of investment, which holds back capital accumulation, growth, and productivity. 
Compared to the U.S., the investment gap is very large (Figure 1): while U.S. fixed investment has increased by a 
factor of 2  
compared to the beginning of the millennium, Germany’s has increased only by 
 20%
. Even compared to 
its EU peers such as France, Sweden, or even the eurozone average, however, German investment lags.  
What’s more, German investment has declined outright since the pandemic. 
 Between 2019 and 2025, aggregate 
investments in Germany declined by 
 -
6.4%, compared with +6.7% in the rest of the Eurozone and +20.2% in the U.S. 
which alone leaves an investment gap of around EUR390bn versus Europe or EUR730bn vs the U.S. Foreign 
investor
s are not closing this gap. 
 T
he number of foreign investment projects announced in Germany fell by 10% in 
2025 to 548, the lowest level since 2009.
 2 
The decline was smaller than in France (
 -
17%) and the UK (
 -
14%
) but  
compared with 2019 Germany has lost more: 
 -
44%, against 
 -
28% in France and 
 -
34% in the UK (Figure 2). Unlike in 
France and the UK, the number has fallen every year since 2019.  
Foreign companies are nevertheless well represented in Germany. The stock of inward FDI stood at 
 EUR
 1.
0
tn at the 
end of 2024.
 3 
The German investment promotion agency GTAI recorded 1,564 foreign investment projects in 2025, 
9.3% fewer than in 2024 but a smaller decline than in the EU (
 -
18.1%) and worldwide (
 -
9.5%).
4

The composition of investment is skewed away from productivity
 -
enhancing items.  
Germany is a laggard in capex 
related to information and communication technology (ICT 
 – 
Figure 3), with ICT
 -
related investment less than half 
the OECD average as a share of GDP. These are investment items that are crucial determinants of productivity

 th.

External shocks don’t explain investment underperformance. 
 External shocks 
 – 
 such as the 2007
 -
08 financial 
crisis, Covid
 -
19, Russia’s full
 -
scale invasion of Ukraine, or the Iran war 
 – 
no doubt played a role. Energy price shocks 
have suppressed investment in Germany’s energy
 -
intensive industrial sectors. However, g
 iven that Germany’s 
investment underperformance is chronic, and that its peers were subject to the same shocks, these shocks only offer 
a partial explanation for Germany’s lack of investment.

### Page 5

5

Figure 4: Gross investment versus gross saving in % of GDP (2024): Germany saves but does not invest.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Bird’s eye view: plenty of saving, not enough investment. 
 Germany’s lack of investment is particularly noteworthy 
given the fact that the economy generates large saving flows (Figure 4). National income accounting identities tell 
us that the gap between saving (S) and investment (I) is an economy’s current accoun
 t (CA) balance: CA = S 
 – 
I or, 
when abstracting from income flows on foreign assets, its export surplus.
 5  
Because the identity holds for the economy as a whole and for each sector, we can trace the surplus to its origins by 
looking at the saving
 -
investment balance of households, government, and financial and non
 -
financial corporations 
(NFC), which together add  
up to the current account (Figure 5). It turns out that, in addition to households’ persistent 
surplus of saving over investment, NFC have also been exhibiting saving surpluses. The government now invests 
more than it saves, after having made positive con
 tributions to the CA balance over much of the 2010s. In turn, CA 
surpluses imply capital exports: the excess of saving over investment is simply invested abroad.  
Figure 5: Saving net of investment by sector, and current account balance (% of GDP): Households have not been 
the only savers

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

5 
Ignoring primary income on net foreign assets and international transfers, the national income identity can be written as Y =  
C + G + I + NX, 
that is, GDP equals the sum of consumption, government spending, investment, and net exports. This can be rearran
 ged to give net exports as 
the difference between saving and investment NX = Y 
 – 
(C + G) 
 – 
I = S 
– 
I since national saving is the excess of GDP over domestic demand from 
consumption and government spending S = Y 
 – 
(C + G). In fact, Germany’s primary income  
balance (income earned on net foreign assets) is 
now almost as large as net exports
 . 
See Bundesbank (2026), Germany’s balance of payments in 2025, Monthly Report, March 2026.

### Page 6

6

Large foreign assets, poor returns  
Germany’s net foreign assets are 87% of GDP
 . Germany has amassed a large amount of claims against the rest of 
the world: its net international investment position (NIIP) 
 – 
 the difference between foreign assets and foreign 
liabilities 
 – 
is about 87% of GDP (Figure 6). From a macroeconomic perspectiv
 e, capital exports can in principle be 
beneficial for a country if it earns higher returns abroad than at home: the proof of the pudding is in the eating.  
Figure 6: Germany’s Net International Investment Position (USD bn): Savings go abroad.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Returns on foreign assets: low 
 – 
and inferior to domestic returns
 . Germany has earned a poor return on its foreign 
assets for decades. Since 1975, its average annual foreign returns were about 5 percentage points below those of 
the U.S. and close to 3 points below those of other European countries (Table 1). What’s more
 , Germany’s foreign 
asset returns were considerably lower than the returns on its domestic assets: domestic saving has been 
systematically directed to foreign assets that earn less than the investment opportunities available at home.
 6 
Table 1: Returns on foreign assets 1975-2020 by country: Germany showing subdued returns on its foreign 
investments.

Source: Hünnekes et al. (2025). 
This suggests that Germany’s current account surplus 
 – 
or its export surplus 
 – 
turns out not to be a “good deal.” 
Instead, more investment at home would be optimal from a macroeconomic perspective. But it also begs the 
question: what are the reasons for Ge
 rmany’s excess saving?

### Page 7

7

The 
 multiple 
 origins of chronic investment shortage  
Households are 
 risk
 -
averse.  
 Germany’s high level of saving is usually attributed to prudent, highly risk
 -
averse 
households. Indeed, German households’ saving rate out of disposable income (Figure 7) is high in international 
comparison. At the same time, it is “natural” for the house
 hold sector in aggregate to feature a surplus of saving 
over investment. Households face an uncertain income stream but wish to smooth consumption. Consequently, they 
spread resources over a lifetime through saving  
making household surpluses a common feature of most economies.

Figure 7: Household gross saving in % of disposable income (2025): German households are heavy savers.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Together with other factors such as risk aversion, the lack of a “Kapitalmarktkultur” (“capital markets culture”) and 
financial literacy
 7
, the institutional setup of pensions provision affects household saving incentives substantially: The 
public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go pension system reduces the need for households to bear market risk for retirement provision. 
As a result, German households hold 37%  
of their financial assets in bank deposits (Figure 8).  
Figure 8: Asset classes as % of total gross financial assets: Bank deposits still a key saving vehicle

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
In turn, banks intermediate those savings in bank deposits into low
 -
risk, low
 -
return assets: corporate loans, which 
favor incumbent firms (due to established relationships) rather than new, innovative firms; and government bonds 
(due to favorable regulator
 y treatment of banks’ holdings). As a result, savings contribute little to the creation of 
risk
-
bearing capital, and available funds for startups and scaling is scarce.

### Page 8

8

Figure 5 suggests that, in addition to households, two more sectors have in recent years tended to have a surplus of 
saving over investment: the general government 
 (
federal, state, and local government together with social security 
funds
 )
, and non
 -
financial corporations.  
The lack of government investment contributed to a lack of demand and poor infrastructure. 
 Government 
surpluses of saving over investment partly reflected a lack of public investment. Between 2002 and 2015 net 
government investment (gross investment less depreciation on the public capital stock) was zero, on average 
(Figure 9). The government ba
 rely invested enough to keep the public capital stock from shrinking.  
Only since 2015 gross investment has been sufficient to keep net investment positive. At 3.5% of GDP in 2025, 
government gross investment  
is now catching up with peers. As a result of this lack of government investment, 
Germany’s infrastructure has deteriorated, which is likely to have contributed to the lack of domestic investment by 
firms.
8  
Figure 9: General government gross vs net investment in % of GDP: Improvement underway

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Corporate surpluses persist.  
Figure 5 shows that the non
 -
financial corporate (NFC) sector has exhibited a surplus 
of saving over investment since around the turn of the millennium. Corporate saving is  
simply retained earnings, i.e. 
profits kept in the firm after paying interest, taxes and dividends. These surpluses became substantial after the great 
financial crisis and have persisted since at macro
 -
significant levels (on average since 2010: 1.6 % of G
 DP; 2025 still 
1.1% of GDP). While an excess of saving over investment is “natural” for households, it is not necessarily the case for 
NFCs. Firms invest to earn  
returns and will usually borrow to invest (or issue equity). Figure 10 shows the persistent 
shortfall of NFC investment vs saving.  
Figure 10: German non-financial corporations, gross saving vs gross investment, % of GDP: The gap persists

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

There are several reasons for the NFC surplus.  
• 
 Wage moderation and strong external demand
 . Real wages in the NFC sector have lagged productivity 
for nearly two decades (Figure 11). Only recently has real compensation for employees caught up. All else 
equal, wages lagging productivity would have boosted firms’ profits. Strong external demand, i
 f not 
accompanied by a commensurate increase in investment spending, will do the same. As shown in Figure 
1
0
, gross NFC saving jumped during periods of strong global demand (2009
 -
11 and after the pandemic, 
2020
 -
22). 
• 
 Lack of domestic demand
 . Wage moderation and a lack of public investment contributed to sluggish 
domestic demand, in turn suppressing economic growth. One consequence was that it made investment in 
faster
 -
growing overseas markets more attractive.  
• 
 Tax incentives favoring retained earnings.  
German corporate tax reforms (2001, 2008) made retaining 
profits more attractive than paying dividends, lowering payout rates and raising NFC gross saving.
 9 
These 
incentives matter in Germany because a large share of corporate profits are generated by privately held 
firms. In privately held firms, owners’ personal tax incentives, rather than pure profit maximization, may 
shape payout decisions. As the additio
 nal saving was not absorbed by investment (Figure 10), it resulted in 
higher net lending.  
Figure 11: Real compensation vs real value added per hour in the non -financial corporate sector (index, 2000 = 
100): wage restraint has contributed to non-financial sector surpluses until recently

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Economic theory suggests that years of underinvestment should raise expected returns and attract capital. In 
Germany, however, a range of supply
 -
side constraints is preventing this adjustment from taking place. 
 Energy 
costs remain elevated following the loss of cheap Russian pipeline gas and the shift towards more expensive LNG 
imports, compounded by high taxes and network charges. Businesses surveyed by EY in 2026 also see the cost of 
energy as Europe’s main dis
 advantage as an investment location, although 60% of them expect Europe’s 
attractiveness to improve over the next three years.
 10 
Labor costs are also burdened by Germany's exceptionally 
high tax wedge, the second highest in the OECD. Bureaucracy further weighs on competitiveness. According to the 
ifo economic research institute, excessive administrative requirements reduce annual o
 utput by up to EUR 146bn, 
or roughly 4% of GDP.  
11 
Although Germany ranks 12th out of 45 countries for overall business attractiveness, it ranks 
second
 -
to
-
last on costs.  
12 
Other structural weaknesses are equally apparent. Demographic decline is reducing the 
supply of skilled labor, while transport, logistics and digital infrastructure bear the consequences of years of 
underinvestment. Financing conditions are less favorable  
than in France or Italy, despite lower German risk
 -
free 
rates, with the fragmented banking system likely playing a role. These long
 -
standing challenges are now being 
compounded by structural shocks. Competition from China has intensified to the point that  
Germany runs a negative 
trade balance with China even in capital goods, for decades its core strength. Meanwhile, parts of the automotive 
sector misjudged both technology and market positioning, favoring combustion engines and fuel cell technology

over affordable electric vehicles. As a result, the higher returns that theory predicts after prolonged underinvestment 
are being eroded by a combination of cost pressures, institutional inefficiencies and structural headwinds.

What needs to be done: Germany’s fiscal push is a first step, but private capital needs to 
follow  
Germany’s fiscal push heralds potential investment turnaround. 
 Yet at least some of the demand
 -
side reasons for 
Germany’s large surpluses of saving over investment are on the mend. The German government has put in place a 
large fiscal expansion centered on infrastructure capex and defense. 
 Following the reform of its debt brake in 2025, 
public deficits are expected to rise from around 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP in the next years, funding hundreds of billions 
of euros in additional spending on infrastructure, defense, energy, climate mitigation & ad
 aption and digitalization
 . 
The government’s fiscal package thus promises to kill two birds with one stone when it comes to improving German 
investment perspectives. In the short run it boosts investment spending, while in the longer run it improves public 
infrastructure, spurring 
 private investment demand.  
Government money alone is not enough: attracting private capital is necessary.  
Closing Germany’s investment 
gap will require more than public funding. The scale of the country’s investment needs is such that substantial 
private capital must also be mobilized. Foreign capital is already responding to some extent. 
 B
ased on Bundesbank 
data, FDI inflows rose by about 50% to around EUR86bn in 2025, after a fall of almost 32% in 2024  
(Figure 1
 2
)
.
13 
Investors from the UK raised their investment by 284% to EUR26bn and are now the largest single source (31% of 
the total), while U.S. investment fell by 44% to EUR11.8bn. Other EU countries contributed EUR43bn, so European 
investors account for more than 
 80% of the total.  
Figure 12: Foreign direct investment inflows to Germany, EUR bn and % of GDP: Rebound in the making

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Germany's challenge is not a shortage of capital but rather directing this capital towards productive investment.  
German households hold around EUR9trn in financial assets, a large proportion of which, by international 
standards, sits in cash and bank deposits (EUR3.4trn).
 14
. Meanwhile, the country's net foreign asset position exceeds 
EUR4.0trn (87% of GDP).  Unlocking even a small portion of these savings could have a transformative impact. 
Shifting just 5% of German households' financial assets out of deposits into more pro
 ductive assets would mobilize 
roughly EUR450bn of investment capital. In addition, repatriating for example 10% of the country’s net foreign 
investments would translate to EUR400bn of additional domestic capital.  
Making Germany more attractive for private investment starts with raising expected returns.  
 Years of 
underinvestment have already increased the potential returns on capital, as scarcity raises the marginal product of 
capital. But unlocking this opportunity requires reducing regulatory barriers, expanding standardized investment 
structures and st
 rengthening the investment culture through tax incentives, pension and insurance reforms, public
 -
private partnerships standards and strategic investment funds. Bottlenecks such as permitting, procurement, deal 
structuring and regulatory friction must be re
 duced. A key enabler for all relevant investment opportunities is

simplification: permitting and procurement procedures need to be accelerated and harmonized to reduce lead 
times. The rapid build
 -

quick infrastructure investments.  
The newly set up “Deutschlandfonds,” with its EUR30bn guarantees and equity targeting to mobilize EUR130bn 
in private capital, is an important step in the right direction  
as it directly targets the gap between Germany's

“Water, water, everywhere, nor any drop to drink”: Germany’s intermediation problem.  
The issue of available 
saving versus domestic investment 
 – 
or the country’s current account surplus 
 – 
is in large part about 
 where  
Germany 
invests. A related, but separate, question is how the savings that stay domestic are used. It turns out that another 
factor holding back investment in Germany is the intermediation of existing savings. Germany, like many other EU 
countries, is par
 ticularly lacking in long
 -
term capital due to the underdevelopment of pension funds (Figure 1
 3
), 
since public pay
 -

 15 
Key reform: towards a more funded retirement system
 . Scandinavia and the Netherlands, economies with similar 
“culture” to Germany, have much higher stock market capitalization as a share of GDP not least because households 
accumulate claims on private pensions, which in turn recycle savings into the domest
 ic equity market (Figure 1
 4
).
16 
The development of pension funds is the key reform because they are upstream of venture capital and private 
equity in the financing chain.  
Figure 13: Insurance and pensions assets as % of nominal GDP, 2025: underdeveloped in Germany

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
The pension commission’s proposal for a funded “Kapitalrente” financed by an additional 2% contribution 
starting 
 from 2028 and invested in a public fund alongside private alternatives, would be a major step in the 
right direction
 .
17 
It would add a capital
 -
funded component to the pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go system in pillar 1 and channel 
household retirement saving into equity markets at scale. According to estimates, the public fund could reach 
around EUR560bn by 2040 (8% of GDP) and become one of  
the world’s largest pension funds within two decades.
 18 
This would create a domestic institutional investor base that sits upstream from venture capital and private equity.  
As an additional pillar 3 contribution to Germany's pension system, the forthcoming “Altersvorsorgedepot” can 
also strengthen domestic investment.  
This reform, which has already been passed, will replace the Riester pension 
from 1 January 2027. Savers will be able to invest in shares, funds and ETFs without a capital guarantee
 . 
The state 
will pay up to 50 cents for every euro saved, up to a total of 360 euros, and an additional 25 cents for every euro 
saved up to a total of 1,800 euros. This results in a maximum annual subsidy of 540 euros.

How to invest in Germany: focus on key investment themes and incentives  
From underinvestment to investment opportunity 
 – 
why now is the right time to close Germany’s up to EUR730bn 
investment gap.  
Germany's investment gap presents both a problem and an opportunity. Years of underinvestment 
have created enormous modernization needs and, in principle, high marginal returns to new capital. Until now, 
structural bottlenecks prevented investors from cap
 turing those returns. The combination of fiscal expansion, reform 
and public
 -
private risk sharing could now begin to change that equation.  The key question that arises next is where 
and how to invest. The answer can be found in four key areas: energy, dig
 ital infrastructure, transport and mobility 
and defense.  
Energy transition:  
The move towards net
 -
zero emissions and greater geopolitical resilience will require substantial 
investment in renewable energy, electricity grids, storage solutions and hydrogen infrastructure. Germany will 
require around EUR1.3tn in investments for rene
 wables, grid infrastructure, storage and hydrogen by 2045, which 
is equivalent to 30% of GDP in 2025.  Financing vehicles and project pipelines are key enablers. However, with 
around 11,000 local authorities and 800 municipal utilities (“Stadtwerke”), indi
 vidual projects are often too small to 
attract institutional investors. To address this, municipalities and regions should be supported through advisory 
services, standardized tender frameworks and training programs to facilitate the structuring of investa
 ble public
 -
private partnerships. Greater participation by districts (“
 Regierungsbezirke”) and counties (“Kreise”) would facilitate 
project aggregation and create scalable investment pipelines. Foreign investors are showing interest here: 
according to EY, the number of low
 -
carbon energy FDI projects in Europe rose by 25% in 2
 025.
21 
Digital infrastructure:  
 Digitalization and digital infrastructure are critical drivers of productivity growth. It is 
estimated that a cumulative investment of between EUR275bn and 500bn will be required by 2030, equivalent to 
6
–
11% of GDP in 2025. This includes spending on compu
 ter hardware, telecommunications equipment, software 
and AI
 -
related infrastructure, such as data centers. Grid access and permitting reform are key enablers. Germany 
should therefore prioritize grid
 -
connection approvals and permitting procedures for data c
 enters and digital 
infrastructure, treating computing capacity with the same urgency as energy security. Multi
 -
year connection queues 
have become the primary constraint on Germany’s AI and digital expansion, rather than a lack of capital or demand. 
Foreign  
capital is already 
 committing: Google plans to invest EUR5.5bn in Germany until 2029, Microsoft announced

19 
Indefi (2026): The German Pension Reform and the Shape of Things to Come  
20 
In addition, the “Frühstartrente” would extend capital
 -
funded provision to children: the state pays 
 EUR
 10 monthly into a depot from age six 
until 18. 
However, the resulting macroeconomic investment volume would be lower than that of the other additional reforms. The Bundesta
 g is 
set to vote on this measure on 16 October 2026.

EY (2026), European Attractiveness Survey, June 2026. The figures for low

EUR3.2bn in 2024 and Nvidia is part of a EUR1bn data center project with Deutsche Telekom in Munich.

AI

FDI projects in Europe rose by 96% in 2025, and the number also went up in Germany although total FDI projects 
declined.  
Transport and mobility: 
 Modernizing transport infrastructure is another major investment priority. The Ministry of 
Transport plans to invest EUR166bn between 2025 and 2029, including EUR107bn for rail, EUR52bn for federal 
roads, and EUR8bn for waterways. A key enabler is performa
 nce
-
based concessions. A new generation of public
 -
private partnerships (PPP 2.0) should be based on performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term 
offtake contracts that link investor returns to measurable and predictable cash flows.  
Defense: 
 German defense financing is expected to reach approximately EUR734bn over five years, enabling the 
country to meet NATO's target of spending 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2029. Planned procurement includes 
land systems, air power, air and missile defense,  
long
 -
range strike capabilities, unmanned systems, munitions, and 
space security assets. Foreign investors are also moving in: the number of defense FDI projects in Europe rose by 
84% in 2025 to 107.
 23 
Only seven of them were in Germany, against 20 in the UK, 14 in France, 12 in Ukraine, 10 in 
Spain and nine in Poland (Figure 14).  Key enabler: simplification. As in other sectors, reducing regulatory and 
procurement complexity will be crucial to acceler
 ating investment and delivery.

What is at stake  
Germany has a rare opportunity to transform today's fiscal stimulus into a sustained investment boom. 
 If 
successful, the country could benefit significantly from a renewed capital influx. Not only would it lift growth from 
the demand side in the short run, it would also raise potential growth and boost long
 -
term growth for decades. The 
benefits would exten
 d beyond Germany 
 – 
Europe cannot afford for its largest economy to remain its weakest link.  
The gains would be economic and political. 
 Stronger growth, functioning infrastructure and rising prosperity would 
help to restore confidence in institutions, strengthening the foundations of the social state and reducing some of the 
economic drivers behind the current political polarization. Howev
 er, successful and bold reforms, as well as 
investors willing to support Germany's renewed momentum, are equally essential to achieving this outcome.  
Failure would come at a high cost. 
 While fiscal spending could provide a temporary boost, structural weaknesses 
would persist, leaving Germany with higher debt and little improvement in long
 -
term growth. The consequences 
would extend beyond the economic sphere, including continued dependenc
 e on foreign energy and technology, 
further erosion of Europe's stability and competitiveness, and continued political polarization and populism. 
Germany's investment challenge is therefore about much more than just growth. It concerns the future economic 
and political resilience of Germany and Europe as a whole.

### Page 1

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: getting the balance right
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
10:46 October 16 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
Australia’s key data release this week was the September Labour Force Survey. Employment surprised
to the downside, -39.3k. However, a 0.2ppt decline in labour force participation from August’s near-
historic high largely offset. The unemployment rate therefore held at 4.7% after August was revised up.

Residual seasonality may be in the mix. Regardless, the take home point from the update is that the

### Page 2

labour market is continuing to ease as employment growth runs below working-age population growth.
We expect the unemployment rate to grind higher into 2027 as tighter financial conditions and cost-of-
living pressures restrict activity growth to a below-trend pace. Although, our forecast peak is near at
4.8%, due by Q2 2027.
This update on the labour market complicates the communication challenge for the RBA. The
September minutes were firmly on the hawkish side, with the discussion focussing on materialising

upside risks to inflation. But, with the unemployment rate now through the RBA’s NAIRU assumption of
4.6%, Governor Bullock’s “a little tight” descriptor for the labour market is becoming harder to defend.
Still, with a clear focus on inflation risks – which also led us to revise up our near-term CPI forecasts in
the October Market Outlook – we still expect a 25bp rate hike at the November meeting.
Over in the US, the headline CPI rose 0.6% as expected in September, taking the annual rate up to

3.7%yr. Energy prices were a key contributor (5.2%, 20.4%yr), while core inflation was a touch above
consensus in the month at 0.3% (2.6%yr). Within the core components, core goods prices gained 0.3%
after being broadly unchanged over the past 6 months (0.7%yr). Core services meanwhile rose 0.4%
(3.1%yr) on a 0.4% increase in shelter (3.2%yr), a 0.6% gain for transport services and a 0.3%
increase in medical care services (2.7%yr and 2.5%yr respectively). Further, the subsequent release of

the PPI pointed to continued upstream price pressures, with the headline and core measures up
6.3%yr and 5.0%yr respectively. The latest Beige Book provided colour on these developments, firms
across multiple districts citing tariffs, insurance and commodity prices as ongoing sources of cost
pressure. Guiding on the risks, many respondents expect further price increases as contracts/hedges
roll off.
Given their telegraphed concerns over inflation, there is enough signal in these reports to warrant a

follow-up hike at the October FOMC meeting. Though it seems Committee members believe they are
near the end of the tightening cycle and so have time on their side. This week, Daly and Bowman both
showed patience in assessing the trend, with no clear bias towards an October or December decision.
Williams stated he believes one more hike would prove sufficient in this cycle given known information.
And Waller also showed confidence, highlighting that elevated long-term yields are doing some of the

FOMC’s work, restricting demand in interest rate sensitive sectors. We hold a similar view, anticipating
that one more hike by year end and a little time will bring inflation near the 2.0%yr medium-term target.
However, as discussed in this month’s Market Outlook, capacity pressures are likely to see inflation
consistently above target, rather than below, in coming years – particularly if, as we suspect, capacity
in the housing sector remains tight. This will mean the FOMC have to remain modestly restrictive over

the forecast horizon and will also bias up long-term yields.
In Asia, there was one release that stood out, China’s trade account. At USD133.5bn, the September
result was a whisker inside January 2025’s record high of USD137.9bn. More impressively, throughout
2025 and 2026 year-to-date the surplus has now averaged USD100.9bn, almost 3 times the average of
2019, prior to the pandemic. The geographic breadth of export growth is as impressive as the headline

annual gain of 36.3%yr, even more so given the ongoing strength in Chinese investment in
neighbouring nations’ production chains. In time, both trade and income receipts from offshore
investment should buoy the renminbi, although for now continued outbound foreign investment should
moderate the pace of gains against other nations in the region, and likely versus the US dollar.

### Page 3

Related articles
Another key trend assessed in October’s Market Outlook is the evolution of producer prices across
Asia, and the likelihood of the disinflationary benefit the west has received in recent decades
continuing. Historically, labour cost arbitrage was one of the key drivers of declining goods prices.
While still possible, we expect the next phase of manufacturing development to instead be driven by
capital, technology and energy. Many end users and the manufacturers undertaking production are

also increasingly prioritising resilience, security and specialisation over cost minimisation. It is therefore
more probably future supply-chain developments will help contain rising costs through productivity than
drive sustained price declines for the goods bought by consumers and businesses.

Document 278: Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds

Source PDF: all report pdf/Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

Compelling risk-reward in 
US 10-year bonds  
Summary 
Event: The US 10 -year government bond yield has surged 
past 5.0% to a 19-year high and has now entered our three -
month forecast range of 5.0-5.25%. At the time of writing, the 
10-year yield is trading at 5.2%, its highest since June 2007’s 
high of 5.32%. The next resistance levels are the 2002 high 
of 5.47% and the 2001 high of 5.53%.  
We view the risk/reward for the US 10-year government 
bond after the recent yield spike as  compelling and we 
are initiating a long opportunistic idea at the current 
yield. As the table on the right shows, if the 10 -year yield 
rises to 6%, investors would face less than 1% in negative 
total returns over a one-year period, whereas if the yield falls 
below 5.0%, they would gain at least 6% in total returns.  
While we continue to prefer the 3-7-year bond maturity range 
for income generation (the US 5-year bond yield is also 
above 5%), and broadly prefer corporate over government 
bonds amid solid corporate fundamentals, the US 10 -year 
government bond yield has finally reached reasonable levels 
for investors looking for slightly longer maturities. 
Rationale: We expect the US 10-year government bond yield 
to ease to 4.75-5.0% over a 12-month period due to: 
Easing oil prices and inflation over next 12 months: While 
the yield is likely to remain elevated in the next three months 
due to elevated oil prices caused by geopolitical uncertainty, 
heavy AI investment-driven bond issuance and a resilient US 
economy, it is likely to ease towards the 4.75 -5.0% range 
over 12  months as oil prices moderate either due to a 
resolution in the Middle East or a rerouting of Middle East 
energy supplies and as the impact of tariffs fade.  
We expect s hallower Fed rate hikes than market s: We 
expect a total of 50bps of Fed rate hikes by June 2027, before 
a cut in H2 as US inflation eventually eases towards the Fed’s 
2% target. This  rate trajectory is shallower than c. 95bps of 
further hikes priced by markets over the next 12 months. 
 The benchmark US 10-year government bond yield has 
risen to its highest since 2007, offering medium-term 
investors a compelling risk-reward trade-off 
US 10-year government bond yield and previous highs

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered

offers investors compelling risk-reward trade off

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Investor positioning adjustment: In the very near-term, the 
US 10 -year bond yield has room to move lower as hedge 
funds using systematic strategies (CTAs) are extremely short 
US government bonds, raising the chance of short -covering 
if upcoming jobs and inflation data miss estimates. 
A shallow Fed hiking cycle  
We expect the shallowest Fed hiking cycle in almost 
three decades. This should enable the US 10-year yield to 
ease towards 4.75 -5.0% over a 12 -month period. Following 
this month’s increase, we forecast two further 25bp hikes by 
June 2027, followed by a cut in H2 2027. Markets price at 
least three more hikes by June and a strong probability of a 
fourth by September. Our projected 75bp s of total hikes this 
cycle compares with 525bps of hikes in 2022–23, 225bps in 
2015–18, 425bps in 2004–06 and 175bp in 1999–2000. The 
last smaller mid-cycle move was a single 25bps hike in 1997. 
Why we expect a more cautious Fed 
The latest hike restored the Fed’s credibility after renewed 
oil-price pressures, elevated inflation and a balanced labour 
market prompted a unanimous policy vote. However, the 
following factors argue against aggressive tightening: 
Supply-driven inflation: Energy costs and AI-infrastructure 
constraints, rather than excess demand, are driving prices. 
Higher rates cannot repair supply and may weaken growth. 
Labour is no longer the main inflation source:  Labour 
supply and demand are better balanced, wage growth has 
moderated and unemployment is near full employment. AI 
further weakens the traditional Phillips Curve relationship 
between unemployment and inflation. 
Disinflation should resume:  Energy pressures may fade 
after the US mid-term elections, while the one-off inflationary 
effects of tariffs should diminish after 2025–26. 
AI raises the neutral rate , but dampens inflation:  
Investment and productivity may lift the neutral rate ( r*), the 
rate consistent with stable inflation and full employment, 
while efficiency gains limit wage-price spirals. 
Markets are already tightening  financial conditions : 
Higher bond yields have raised borrowing costs and 
restrained activity, doing part of the Fed’s work. 
The cycle began from a higher base:  The Fed funds rate 
started at 3.75%, versus 0.25% in the previous two cycles 
and 1% before that, reducing additional tightening required. 
Monetary transmission has weakened:  Wealthier 
households drive more spending but are less rate -sensitive, 
while lower -income consumers are already constrained. 
Further hikes may , therefore, curb demand less effectively 
while causing disproportionate harm to low-income families. 
 We expect a shallower Fed rate hiking cycle compared 
with market expectations

### Page 3

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Small business conditions are deteriorating:  Higher 
financing and energy costs are weakening sentiment, as 
reflected in the latest Beige Book . The renewed 
underperformance of US small capitalisation (S&P 600) and 
mid capitalisation (S&P 400) equity indices  versus the large 
cap S&P 500 index over the past couple of months also show 
rising pressure on small businesses. 
Fiscal and financial -stability risks are rising:  Aggressive 
Fed tightening would increase US debt-servicing costs as the 
US Treasury seeks cheaper funding through shorter-maturity 
issuance, while potentially amplifying financial-system stress. 
Upside risk to bond yields 
Our view of lower bond yields carries the following risks: 
Persistent inflation:  Repeated supply shocks, strong AI -
related investment demand and tighter immigration controls 
could keep inflation above target and force steeper Fed rate 
hikes. With unemployment near the Fed’s estimate of full 
employment, a sharp drop in labour supply could intensify 
wage pressures and destabilise inflation expectations . We 
note that long-term expectations remain contained for now. 
Geopolitical escalation:  An intensification of geopolitical 
conflicts after the mid -term elections could lift energy prices 
and inflation. However, a Democrat -controlled or divided 
Congress may constrain US foreign-policy initiatives through 
tighter budgets, while Republicans could turn less supportive 
as attention shifts to the 2028 presidential race. 
Higher neutral rate: AI investments could raise the neutral 
rate (r*), turning current monetary policy settings 
accommodative. This could necessitate further rate hikes. 
However, the Fed has barely raised its nominal neutral rate 
estimate, lifting it to 3.2% in September from 3.0% a year ago. 
Bottomline: The outlook for rates remains geopolitics 
dependent, with all Fed meetings live . However, based on 
our core scenario, we see the US 10-year yield settling lower 
in the 4.75-5.0% in 6-12 months. A shallow Fed hiking cycle 
due to expected disinflation offers investors a reasonably 
attractive risk-reward in US 10-year government bonds. 
US 30-year government bonds , on the other hand, may 
continue to see elevated yields as the US fiscal deficit is 
unlikely to have improved next year, and may look much 
worse. As a result, “term premium” may keep the ultra -long 
end elevated. Short of some unorthodox policy to artifici ally 
cap them lower, the 30-year yield is likely to continue to drift 
higher towards the 5.5-5.75% range into H2 2027. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist 
Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

Document 284: CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng hóa Sài Gòn - Báo cáo cập nhật 2Q2026 (2026-09-22)

### Page 1

CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng hóa Sài Gòn(SCS) 
Thách thức từ Long Thành 
22/09/2026  
Chuyên viên cao cấp Nguyễn Ngọc Anh 
(+84) 24-7303-5333 anhntn@kbsec.com.vn

KQKD 2Q2026 giảm nhẹ

Mạng bay Trung Đông phục hồi hỗ 
trợ sản lượng SCS từ 2H2026

Long Thành tạo áp lực lên sản lượng 
và biên lợi nhuận từ 2027, triển vọng 
dài hạn phụ thuộc khả năng tham 
gia vận hành nhà ga mới

Cổ tức 2026 dự kiến ở mức 5,000 
VND/cổ phiếu

Khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, giá mục 
tiêu 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu

2Q2026, SCS ghi nhận doanh thu 279 tỷ VND (-4% yoy), LNST đạt 188 tỷ VND (đi 
ngang svck). Lũy kế 1H2026, doanh thu đạt 551 tỷ VND (-1% yoy), LNST đạt 361 tỷ 
VND (+0.5% yoy), trong đó thu nhập tài chính tăng mạnh bù đắp phần lớn sự suy 
giảm lợi nhuận từ hoạt động khai thác nhà ga.

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng mùa cao điểm hàng hóa cuối năm và mạng bay Trung Đông 
phục hồi sẽ hỗ trợ sản lượng SCS trong những tháng cuối năm. Tuy nhiên, mức độ 
cải thiện còn hạn chế do nhu cầu vận tải trên các tuyến Trung Đông – châu Á và 
châu Âu – Trung Đông vẫn suy yếu. Sản lượng hàng quốc tế/nội địa năm 2026 dự 
kiến đạt 209/52 nghìn tấn (-7%/-4% yoy).

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sau khi các hạ tầng kết nối với sân bay Long Thành được hoàn 
thiện, tỷ lệ phân bổ chuyến bay quốc tế giữa Long Thành và Tân Sơn Nhất sẽ dao 
động quanh khoảng 75%/25%. Tỷ trọng phân bổ hàng quốc tế tại TP. Hồ Chí Minh 
qua Long Thành 2027/2028/2029 dự kiến sẽ đạt 10%/35%/55%.

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng SCS tiếp tục duy trì cổ tức tiền mặt cao nhờ khả năng tạo dòng 
tiền ổn định và quy mô tiền gửi lớn. Cổ tức năm 2026 dự kiến đạt 5,000 VND/cổ 
phiếu, trước khi duy trì ở mức 3,000 VND/cổ phiếu/năm từ 2027 nhằm tích lũy 
nguồn lực cho các dự án mới.

Dựa trên triển vọng kinh doanh và kết quả định giá, chúng tôi đưa ra khuyến nghị 
TRUNG LẬP với cổ phiếu SCS, mức giá mục tiêu 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu.

của SCS bao gồm CTCP Gemadept (33.4%) 
và Tổng Công ty Cảng Hàng không Việt Nam 
(13.6%).

doanh chính của SCS. Hiện SCS đang tiếp 
tục mua sắm trang thiết bị nâng công suất 
xử lý hàng lên 350,000 tấn/năm.

ngang yoy). Lũy kế 1H2026, doanh thu đạt 551 tỷ VND (-1% yoy), LNST đạt 361

suy giảm lợi nhuận từ hoạt động khai thác nhà ga.

Biểu đồ 4. Cơ cấu thông lượng hàng SCS (tấn)  Biểu đồ 5. Giá dịch vụ và biên LNG hoạt động khai thác nhà ga 
của SCS

Nguồn: SCS  Nguồn: SCS

Mạng bay Trung Đông phục hồi hỗ 
trợ sản lượng SCS trong 2H2026, 
song mức độ cải thiện còn hạn chế 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sản lượng hàng hóa của SCS sẽ cải thiện trong những tháng 
cuối năm 2026 nhờ mạng bay Trung Đông dần được khôi phục, đặc biệt từ 
Qatar Airways, cùng nhu cầu vận tải hàng hóa tăng trong mùa cao điểm cuối 
năm. Tuy nhiên, mức độ phục hồi dự kiến còn hạn chế khi nhu cầu vận tải trên 
các tuyến Trung Đông – Châu Á và Châu Âu – Trung Đông vẫn giảm lần lượt 
14.1% và 16.1% yoy trong tháng 7/2026 (tháng thứ 5 liên tiếp), trái ngược với 
mức tăng 3.9% của thị trường hàng hóa hàng không toàn cầu. Diễn biến này 
phần nào giải thích việc sản lượng hàng quốc tế của SCS tiếp tục giảm khoảng 
10% yoy trong tháng 7–8, dù tần suất bay của các hãng Trung Đông đã phục 
hồi đáng kể.

Chúng tôi dự phóng sản lượng hàng quốc tế và nội địa trong bốn tháng cuối 
năm lần lượt giảm 4% và 2% yoy, cả năm 2026 đạt 209.4 nghìn tấn (-7% yoy) và 
52.2 nghìn tấn (-4% yoy). Doanh thu khai thác nhà ga được kỳ vọng tiếp tục 
được hỗ trợ bởi việc điều chỉnh tăng giá dịch vụ từ đầu năm, bù đắp một phần 
tác động từ sản lượng suy giảm.

Biểu đồ 6. Mức độ phục hồi chuyến bay của các hãng hàng không vùng Vịnh 
Đến ngày 21/09/2026, tần suất bay của các 
hãng vùng Vịnh đã phục hồi đáng kể, phần 
lớn đạt khoảng 85–100% mức trước xung đột, 
riêng Qatar Airways đạt 97%, tăng từ mức 
87% ghi nhận giữa tháng 6.

### Page 5

Long Thành tạo áp lực lên sản 
lượng và biên lợi nhuận từ 2027, 
triển vọng dài hạn phụ thuộc khả 
năng tham gia vận hành nhà ga mới 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng nhu cầu vận tải hàng hóa hàng không khu vực phía Nam duy 
trì tăng trưởng trong trung hạn, với sản lượng hàng quốc tế/nội địa tăng 8%/4% 
yoy năm 2027 và 9%/6% mỗi năm từ 2028, được hỗ trợ bởi hoạt động xuất 
nhập khẩu, thương mại điện tử và sự phát triển của chuỗi cung ứng. Tuy nhiên, 
sản lượng hàng quốc tế qua SCS dự kiến chịu áp lực khi các chuyến bay từng 
bước chuyển sang Long Thành. Trong kịch bản cơ sở, chúng tôi giả định tỷ lệ 
chuyến bay quốc tế chuyển sang Long Thành lần lượt đạt 10%/35%/55%/70% 
trong giai đoạn 2027–2030, với tác động lên sản lượng thực tế phụ thuộc vào 
tiến độ khai thác nhà ga hàng hóa và hệ thống logistics tại sân bay mới.

Đồng thời, giá dịch vụ quốc tế dự kiến bắt đầu giảm từ giữa năm 2027 nhằm giữ 
chân các hãng hàng không, trong khi giá nội địa kỳ vọng duy trì tăng 3%/năm. 
Theo đó, biên lợi nhuận gộp dự kiến giảm nhẹ trong năm 2027 và chịu áp lực 
đáng kể từ 2028 khi tỷ trọng hàng quốc tế có mức giá dịch vụ cao suy giảm.

Bên cạnh đó, SCS có cơ hội mở rộng quy mô khai thác trong dài hạn thông qua 
việc tham gia vận hành nhà ga hàng hóa số 1 tại Long Thành, công suất thiết kế 
550 nghìn tấn/năm. Doanh nghiệp đã ký biên bản ghi nhớ với ACV về khả năng 
tham gia vận hành nhà ga này, qua đó có thể tiếp cận nguồn hàng dịch chuyển 
từ Tân Sơn Nhất nếu được lựa chọn. Tuy nhiên, chúng tôi chưa đưa nguồn thu 
từ dự án vào kịch bản cơ sở khi chưa có kết quả lựa chọn đơn vị vận hành chính 
thức.

Biểu đồ 7. Thông lượng hàng và biên LNG của SCS 2022 – 2028F

Nguồn: SCS, KBSV dự phóng

Nền tảng tài chính lành mạnh và 
chính sách cổ tức tiền mặt ổn định 
hỗ trợ định giá cổ phiếu 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng SCS tiếp tục duy trì chính sách cổ tức tiền mặt ổn định trong 
những năm tới nhờ khả năng tạo dòng tiền cao, quy mô tiền gửi lớn và mức 
đòn bẩy tài chính thấp. Trong 1H2026, thu nhập tài chính tăng mạnh đã bù đắp 
phần lớn sự suy giảm lợi nhuận từ hoạt động khai thác nhà ga, giúp LNST duy 
trì ổn định ở mức 361 tỷ VND (+0.5% yoy). Chúng tôi dự phóng cổ tức tiền mặt 
năm 2026 tiếp tục đạt 5,000 VND/cổ phiếu, trước khi duy trì ở mức 3,000 
VND/cổ phiếu/năm từ 2027, phù hợp với triển vọng lợi nhuận chịu áp lực từ 
Long Thành và nhu cầu tích lũy nguồn lực tài chính để tham gia các dự án mới.

Khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, giá mục 
tiêu 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu  
 Sử dụng phương pháp chiết khấu dòng tiền (DCF), trên cơ sở cập nhật triển 
vọng kinh doanh và tác động từ quá trình chuyển đổi khai thác sang Long 
Thành, chúng tôi đưa ra giá mục tiêu cho SCS là 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu, tương 
đương với mức tiềm năng sinh lời 14.3% so với mức giá đóng cửa ngày 
22/09/2026. Trong kịch bản cơ sở, chúng tôi chưa đưa nguồn thu từ việc tham 
gia vận hành nhà ga hàng hóa số 1 tại Long Thành vào định giá. Các giả định 
chính bao gồm: 
- Thông lượng hàng quốc tế năm 2026 đạt 209.4 nghìn tấn (-7% yoy), với 
bốn tháng cuối năm giảm 4% yoy do mạng bay Trung Đông chưa phục 
hồi hoàn toàn. Nhu cầu hàng quốc tế khu vực phía nam dự kiến tăng 
8% năm 2027 và 9%/năm trong giai đoạn 2028–2030, kéo theo lượng 
hàng qua SCS trong 2027/2028/2029/2030 sẽ giảm lần lượt -2.8%/-
21%/-25%/-27% yoy, trước khi tăng trưởng trở lại từ 2031 sau khi đạt 
tỷ trọng phân bổ tối ưu giữa SGN và LTA. 
- Thông lượng hàng quốc nội năm 2026 đạt 52.2 nghìn tấn (-4% yoy), với 
bốn tháng cuối năm giảm 2% yoy do áp lực chi phí vận tải và điều 
chỉnh mạng bay. Sản lượng dự kiến phục hồi 4% năm 2027 và 6%/năm 
từ 2028 khi chi phí nhiên liệu hạ nhiệt và năng lực khai thác cải thiện. 
- Giá nội địa dự kiến tăng 3%/năm, trong khi giá quốc tế bắt đầu giảm 
từ giữa 2027 nhằm giữ chân các hãng hàng không. Biên lợi nhuận gộp 
dự kiến giảm nhẹ trong 2027 và chịu áp lực đáng kể từ 2028 khi tỷ 
trọng hàng quốc tế suy giảm và chi phí cố định không giảm tương ứng

Document 299: Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Entering the age of the older consumer As America ages, older consumers have more leisure time, significant wealth and growing economic influence. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

13023135     1

Economy 
Entering the age of the older consumer 
01 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• The older consumer is moving center stage. America's population has been aging for decades, but the shift has accelerated: the 
share aged over 60 increased by almost 10 percentage points (pp) between 1995 and 2025. And the Census Bureau projects the 
share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population. 
• More free time means a different spending mix. People aged 65 and over spend around two and a half fewer hours per day 
working and roughly two hours more on leisure than the overall adult population. Bank of America data shows that older 
households allocate relatively more of their card spending to groceries and travel, and less to restaurants, gasoline, general 
merchandise and clothing. 
• Households aged over 55 held close to $140 trillion in net worth in the second quarter of 2026, giving many older consumers 
considerable capacity to spend on travel and other leisure categories. But wealth is not evenly distributed, and healthcare and 
other cost pressures remain significant for older households on tighter or fixed incomes.

Population aging is nothing new, but it is accelerating  
The US population has been growing relatively “older” for a long time. Exhibit 1 shows that the proportion of the population 
under 20 years old shrank from the mid-1960s through 2025. At the same time, the proportion of the population in older age 
groups has been rising.  
Exhibit 1: Over time the US population has steadily aged  
Proportion of US population by age range (%)

Source: Human Mortality Database; Bank of America Institute calculations 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

### Page 2

  I NSTI TUTE

Additionally, there has been a notable acceleration in the relative aging of the population in recent years. For example, between 
1995 and 2025, the proportion of the population aged over 60 rose by almost 10 percentage points (pp) to 25%.  
Several factors have driven this. First, fertility rates in the US, as in much of the world, have fallen sharply. This has slowed 
natural population growth and, by reducing the number of younger people relative to older cohorts, has increased the share of 
the population at older ages. Second, more recently, net inward migration to the US has slowed. Because migrants tend to be 
younger than the overall population, weaker immigration also tends to raise the average age. Finally, rising life expectancy over 
much of the post-war period has allowed more people to survive into older age, further increasing the older population's share.  
Exhibit 2: The trend towards an older composition of the population is expected to continue  
Forecast changes in the share of population by age group relative to 2025 (pp)

Source: United States Census Bureau 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Lower fertility and, more recently, weaker net migration look set to slow overall US population growth in the decades ahead. As a 
result, older Americans are likely to play an increasingly important role in the economy, both as a share of the population and, in 
many age groups, in absolute numbers. The Census Bureau projects the share of the population over 60 will rise another 5pp by 
2055, to stand at nearly 30% of the population (Exhibit 2). For businesses, understanding how the needs and spending patterns 
of older consumers differ from those of younger generations will therefore become increasingly relevant. 
Older age groups allocate their time differently  
Why does this matter? One reason is that older people tend to spend their time and money quite differently from younger adults. 
Most obviously, many work less or have retired altogether. Data from the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) shows that people 
aged 65 and over spend, on average, around two and a half fewer hours per day working than the population aged 15+ (Exhibit 
3). 
That leaves considerably more time for other activities. People aged 65 and over spend around two hours more per day on 
leisure and sports activities, with television viewing accounting for a particularly large share of this additional leisure time 
(Exhibit 4).

Older households also spend differently  
Bank of America internal data on credit and debit card spending provides insights into how these differences in older 
generations’ use of time also impact their spending. Rather than splitting our spending data by generation, as we often do, here 
we split by age bands, allowing for consistent comparisons over time.  
Exhibit 5 shows how the composition of card spending of households aged 61-75 and those older than 75 differs from that of all 
households. A few things stand out. For one, older households devote a greater share of their overall card spending to groceries 
(food and beverages). Households aged 61-75 also allocate more spending to travel (airlines and lodging). And restaurants and 
bars, gasoline, general merchandise and clothing account for a smaller share of older households’ card spending.  
These differences may seem fairly intuitive. Older households have more leisure hours, so spend relatively more on travel to 
enjoy them. Previously we found that more available time may support travel particularly around retirement, although the 
discretionary spending lift can be temporary (for more, read our publication: Paychecks to pensions: The evolution of retiree 
spending).  
Older households are also likely to have less need to update their work wardrobes, so they probably spend less on clothing.

Still, older households’ relatively lower spending on restaurants and bars is a little more curious, perhaps, given dining out could 
be one way of making use of more leisure time. Indeed, when we look at how these spending shares are evolving, we find a 
nuanced picture. While households aged 61-75 do spend relatively less on restaurants and bars than the overall population, they 
appear to be narrowing this gap. But for those older than 75 this is not the case. In our view, the pandemic may still be weighing 
on dining-out spending among this older age group (Exhibit 6).

Aging presents opportunities, but also challenges 
In our view, the aging of the US population presents important opportunities for the economy and businesses. Older consumers 
have more leisure time and businesses that cater to these demands may therefore benefit. 
Moreover, some older consumers hold considerable amounts of net wealth and have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of 
recent rises in equities. Using data from the Federal Reserve, Exhibit 8 shows that as of the second quarter of 2026 the net 
worth of those households aged 55 and over was close to $140 trillion, around three-quarters of the total. And with equity 
markets rising strongly, these age groups have seen a more than 20% increase in their net worth over the past two years, 
potentially enhancing their spending power further.  
But an important caveat is that not all older consumers are in this position. Previous Institute work found evidence consistent 
with equity-market wealth effects being concentrated toward higher-income households (read our publication: Paychecks to 
pensions: The evolution of retiree spending). And Census Bureau research has found that around 14% of Social Security 
recipients aged over 65 rely on it for over 90% of their income. For older households without a wealth cushion, recent elevated 
cost pressures, like gasoline, will likely have put them under more significant financial strain. They will also be more dependent 
on cost-of-living (COLA) increases in Social Security to underpin their spending.  
And, more broadly, outside of card spending, healthcare costs are a much larger factor in older households’ budgets (Exhibit 9). 
The evolution of these costs going forward will be a key variable for older consumers’ budgets.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.
SOURCE: S&P GLOBAL, BNP PARIBAS
Eurozone sectoral PMI indices: production and new export orders are strengthening across more sectors

Chart of the Week / 14 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.

Document 391: Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

Threats to European diesel imports: risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased 
risk of price rises

In Europe, the possibility of a US ban on diesel exports has heightened concerns about price trends for this widely used 
fuel1, which has already risen significantly since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran. It has become a major political issue 
on both sides of the Atlantic in the run-up to decisive elections.

A particularly tight diesel market due to a shortage of buffer stocks 
Following the outbreak of the conflict, diesel prices did indeed see some of the sharpest rises on the oil market due to particularly 
tight market conditions. Since the end of February, whilst the price of a barrel of crude has risen by 46%, diesel prices have soared 
by 100% in Europe and by 76% in the United States.

The factors alleviating pressure on the refined products market, and more specifically the diesel market, were more limited than in 
the case of crude oil. Shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz (around 17% of maritime diesel trade prior to the conf lict) 
have fallen by around 75%. Added to this is the halt in Russian exports due to war damage, which is depriving the diesel market of 
around 12% of its maritime exports. Furthermore, unlike with crude oil, China has not acted as a stabilising  force in the diesel 
market. On the contrary, the energy crisis has led to a halt in Chinese exports of refined products (including diesel) to the  rest of 
Asia from April onwards. The resumption of Chinese exports over the last two months remains fragile, given, in particular, that 
diesel stocks are at their lowest level in 15 months.

The United States has eased these tensions in the diesel market by increasing its exports of refined products (up 25% on average 
in the first half of 2026 compared with the average over the last five years). This rise in exports has required high refinery utilisation 
rate (close to maximum capacity) and, to a lesser extent, a reduction in stocks (-11% compared with the average over the last five 
years as at mid -September for middle distillates 2). All these factors have contributed to tightening the US domestic market and 
pushed retail diesel prices to record levels. Road diesel prices (US average) have risen by 68% since the end of February and by 
74% year-on-year.

A structurally high level of vulnerability in Europe… 
Against this market backdrop, characterised by mounting tensions and very limited room for manoeuvre , Europe would be 
particularly hard hit by a reduction in – or even a complete halt to – US exports. According to Eurostat, imports accounted for 46% 
of European demand for diesel in the first half of 2026, with 40% of these imports coming from the United States. Europe, which

1 Diesel accounts for 17% of the primary energy mix in the EU and 7.3% in the United States. 
2 A category of refined petroleum products comprising mainly diesel and aviation fuel.

### Page 2

has lost a great deal of production capacity in recent years, is operating its refineries at near -maximum capacity. Its room for 
manoeuvre is therefore limited.

… But the risk of a shortage has been ruled out in the short term 
The risk of a shortage, however, appears limited in the short term. The situation regarding European stocks is not comfortable, but 
it is not alarming. Commercial diesel stocks in the ARA region 3 are currently at historically low levels (around 12 million barrels), 
23% below their level at the end of September 2025. As for emergency stocks (EU Emergency Stocks), these are, according to the 
European Commission, at a sufficient level. Eurostat estim ates this figure at 270 million barrels in June 2026 (stable from a year 
earlier), representing approximately 60 days’ worth of EU consumption. Europe imports around 0.9 mb/d of diesel from the United 
States, and emergency stocks would therefore cover around 60 days’ consumption at 4.5 mb/d.

The rise in prices could be significant 
Whatever the outcome of the conflict, diesel prices are likely to continue to rise in the short term. According to our base -case 
scenario, crude oil prices are expected to remain very high in Q4 2026 due to ongoing tensions in the Persian Gulf, whilst a return 
to normal levels of diesel exports from Russia and the Middle East appears unlikely by that time.

Among the factors likely to cause future tensions, the start of the maintenance season in Europe could affect production leve ls, 
whilst demand for diesel in the United States is typically high in the autumn (agricultural harvests and pre -winter storage). I n a 
scenario where tensions remain low, the diesel refining margin (crack spread) could rise by 5% in Q4 compared with its level at the 
end of September 2026, before gradually falling back during 2027. An intermediate scenario (a 10% rise in the crack spread in Q4 
2026) would result in moderate constraints on US diesel exports and a drawdown of European stocks. Finally, an adverse scenario 
(a 35% rise in the crack spread in Q4 2026) would imply severe restrictions on US diesel exports and a delayed European response.

What impact will this have on inflation in the euro area? 
According to our estimates, the rise in diesel prices would exacerbate the expected peak in inflation in the euro area without altering 
its timing. In our baseline scenario for September 2026 (see our recently published EcoPerspective), headline inflation would peak 
at 4.1% y/y in January 2027 before gradually falling back towards 2% by the end of the year. The two least adverse scenarios 
would add only a limited amount to the peak: +0.1 pp in the first scenario (+5% for the crack spread in Q4 compared with the end 
of Q3) and +0.2 pp in the intermediate scenario (+10%), with inflation reaching 4.2% and 4.3% respectively in January 2027. 
However, there is an upside risk in the second scenario linked to the uncertain extent of the spillov er effects. In both cases, the 
main impact would be felt through fuel prices (which quickly reflect rising market prices) and would fade over the course of 2027. 
In the case of the adverse scenario (+35%), the additional inflation would be close to 0.6 pp by early 2027, pushing inflation above 
4.6%. A shock of this magnitude would, in fact, reduce businesses’ ability to absorb rising costs, which would lead to price increases 
across a broader range of goods (food, industrial goods). Subsequently, the effect would only fade gradually.

Pascal Devaux 
Economist, Middle East, Western Balkans – Energy 
pascal.devaux@bnpparibas.com

Guillaume Derrien 
Economist, Europe, Eurozone, International Trade 
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

3 Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp, Europe’s main hub for the storage of raw materials.
